## Full Text

## 1 Introduction

As one type of important inorganic nonmetallic material, ceramic materials are the basic materials for many industrial fields, such as the aerospace, nuclear, electronics, machinery, and chemical industries [1−4]. Among them, ceramic fibers, which have the advantages of being lightweight, having high-temperature resistance, and good chemical stability, are drawing increasing attention [5,6]. Reinforcing the matrix with ceramic fibers to obtain composites is an effective way to improve the fracture toughness of ceramic materials and expand their application scope [7,8]. For example, SiC ceramic fiber-reinforced SiC (SiCf/SiC) composites are assumed to be ideal thermal structural materials for aero-engines, supersonic vehicles, and nuclear reactors [5,9−15]. Therefore, much effort has been devoted to using advanced SiC fibers and other carbide ceramic fibers [1,16−18]. Over the past decade, the development of various ceramic fibers has tremendously enriched the modern family of materials.

In recent years, with the continuous development of the aerospace industry, high-speed vehicles have been facing a more demanding flight environment, requiring structural materials with extraordinary high-temperature resistance [19]. In particular, during the flight of a supersonic vehicle, the nose cone, the front of the wing, and the tail are under intense aerodynamic heating, causing the surface temperature of the vehicle to rise sharply and even reach more than 3000 °C [20]. Therefore, in addition to SiC materials, ultrahigh temperature ceramics (UHTCs) are urgently needed [20−23]. UHTCs mainly consist of transition metal compounds, including oxides (ZrO2 and HfO2) [23,24], borides (ZrB2 and HfB2) [25,26], nitrides (HfN and TiN) [27,28], carbides (ZrC, HfC, and TaC) and their composites [3,29−31]. Among them, transition metal carbide ceramics have become the focus of research due to their ultrahigh melting point, high hardness, appropriate coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE), and high thermal conductivity [11]. The basic properties of some major carbides are listed in Table 1. The preparation of high-performance carbide ceramic fibers is important for extending the application of these carbide materials to various extreme environments.

Numerous methods have been applied for preparing carbide ceramic fibers, significantly affecting the fiber diameter, microstructure, and properties [32,33]. For example, large-diameter (100–150 µm) SiC fibers made by chemical vapor deposition (CVD) exhibit high strength (> 3 GPa) but poor flexibility. As a result, these fibers are unsuitable for weaving and are mainly used as reinforcements for metal matrix composites (MMCs) [34,35]. Fine-diameter SiC fibers (36]. The fine-diameter SiC fibers possess good mechanical properties (tensile strength up to 3 GPa and Young’s modulus up to 400 GPa), with better flexibility than the CVD-SiC fibers. These materials can be braided into various complex shapes according to specific requirements [32]. SiC nanofibers are prepared using the sol–gel/PD method combined with electrospinning. These fibers can be used as both high-temperature-resistant structural and functional materials for potential applications in electrochemistry, catalysis, and sensors [37−41]. To date, among all of the carbide ceramic fibers, only SiC fibers obtained via CVD and PD methods have achieved mass production [32]. For transition metal carbide ceramics (e.g., HfC, TaC, and ZrC), although their particles and whiskers have been prepared [42−44], there are few reports on the fabrication of their fibers.

The diameter of ceramic fibers is vital for their properties, further affecting their final application. The diameter of ceramic fibers directly affects their flexibility, strength, microwave absorption, and thermal insulation properties [37,45−48]. Fibers with large diameters are less flexible, and reducing the diameter of the fibers can effectively increase their flexibility [46,49]. A reduction in the diameter of ceramic fibers could increase their strength by reducing the volume and initial defects (voids and cracks) of the fibers [45,46], which also increases the surface area of the fibers, enriching the fiber-matrix interface and thus improving the mechanical properties of the composite [50]. It has been proven that smaller diameters could improve the microwave absorption capabilities of fibers [51]. Additionally, reducing the diameter of ceramic fibers can decrease their thermal conductivity and infrared transmittance while improving their thermal insulation performance [52,53]. Notably, when the diameter of ceramic fibers is reduced to the nanoscale, they can exhibit the advantages of both structural and functional materials [54]. Overall, it can be concluded that a change in the diameter of carbide ceramic fibers affects their performance [45,48,55].

This review summarizes the current development of carbide ceramic fibers with different fiber diameters, focusing on the preparation of nonmetallic carbide (SiC and B4C) ceramic fibers and transition metal carbide (ZrC, HfC, TaC, and TiC) ceramic fibers with high melting points (> 3000 °C). Furthermore, this review presents an overview of the application of a series of carbide ceramic fibers (Fig. 1). Thus, this review is expected to provide a great theoretical and practical reference for the preparation and application of carbide ceramic fibers, thereby advancing the development of high-performance carbide ceramic fibers.

#### 2.1.1 Large-diameter SiC fibers

Although large-diameter SiC fibers are not easy to weave, they exhibit advantages, including high-temperature resistance, high strength (> 3 GPa), and elastic modulus (400 GPa). The fibers can maintain good mechanical properties above 1200 °C and possess good compatibility with the metal matrix. Therefore, large-diameter SiC fibers are often used as reinforcements for MMCs, such as Ti-based composites [80−82], Ni-based composites [83−85], Cu-based composites [86], and Al-based composites [87]. Among these MMCs, large-diameter SiC fiber-reinforced Ti-based alloys often serve as lightweight structural materials in the fields of high thrust-to-weight ratio engines and hypersonic aircraft [34,88,89]. In other areas, such as automotive and sports equipment, large-diameter SiC fiber-reinforced MMCs are recognized as ideal lightweight high-temperature structural materials, thus allowing for large-scale application. To date, many researchers have studied the fabrication and application of large-diameter SiC fibers [90−94].

Usually, large-diameter SiC fibers are fabricated by the CVD method [90−92]. The fabrication process of large-diameter SiC fibers is as follows: The carbon/tungsten wire is heated to more than 1200 °C by direct current (DC) resistance heating/radio frequency (RF) heating via mercury electrodes; a mixture of CH3SiCl and H2 is introduced to react, and the reaction product SiC is deposited on the scorched core surface [94]. The diameter of most SiC fibers obtained by CVD ranges from 100 to 150 µm. Figure 2 shows a schematic diagram of a two-stage CVD reactor [91]. To further explore the application of these fibers, intensive efforts have been devoted to studying the compatibility between large-diameter SiC fibers and various metal matrices and the properties of their composites [35,55,95−97].

*
Fig. 2Schematic diagram of two-stage CVD reactor for fabricating W-core SiC fibers. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [91], © Elsevier Ltd. 2020.

According to the core materials, large-diameter SiC fibers can be divided into two kinds: C-core SiC fibers, represented by the SCS series, and W-core SiC fibers, represented by the Sigma series [32]. Both series of SiC fibers have been successfully commercialized and widely used [80,98,99]. The SCS series of SiC fibers contains several SiC fibers with different SiC deposition thicknesses and C/Si atomic ratios, effectively influencing their properties. For example, SCS-6 fibers with a diameter of 142 µm have a tensile strength of 4 GPa and an elastic modulus of 400–415 GPa [100]. SCS-ultrafibers (142 µm) possess the same thermal stability and elastic modulus as SCS-6 fibers but a higher tensile strength (6 GPa). When being applied as reinforcement, the difference in the SCS series of SiC fibers mainly determines the mechanical properties of their reinforced composites [55,96,99]. For example, the flexural strength and damage tolerance of SCS-9A fiber-reinforced Ti matrix composites were greater than those of the same matrix reinforced with SCS-6 fibers [55].

Unlike the SCS series with a carbon fiber core (33 µm), the Sigma fibers are obtained by depositing SiC on tungsten wires with a small diameter (15 µm), as shown in Fig. 3 [32]. For example, the tensile strength and elastic modulus of Sigma 1140+ fibers (106 µm) were 4 and 400 GPa, respectively [101]. The structure of large-diameter SiC fibers can be roughly divided into four parts from the fiber center outward: the core, the carbon-rich SiC layer, the SiC layer, and the carbon-rich coating on the outer surface. The strength and modulus of large-diameter fibers mainly depend on their composition and microstructure. The main factors include the integrity of the carbon coating on the outer surface, the spatial variation in the microstructure of the SiC layer, and the interface between the SiC layer and the core.

Currently, the commercially available large-diameter SiC fibers primarily include SCS and Sigma fibers. The microstructure and properties of these large-diameter SiC fibers are shown in Table 2. Furthermore, as depicted in Table 2, several Chinese research groups, such as Northwestern Polytechnical University [90,102−107], the Beijing Institute of Aerospace Research [87,95,108], and the Chinese Academy of Sciences [83,109−111], have investigated the fabrication of large-diameter SiC fibers through CVD. These research groups have investigated W-core SiC fibers, exploring the correlation between process conditions and mechanical properties during the fabrication of CVD-SiC fibers to enhance their performance [90,91,107].

Different deposited substrate materials cause variations in the interface between the fiber core and the SiC layer. The typical C-core SiC fiber, SCS-6 fiber, has received much attention [98−100,113,118]. Guo et al*. [113] used transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HRTEM) to observe the structural changes of SCS-6 fibers. They found a pyrolytic carbon layer around the C core and proved no interfacial reaction between the C-core and SiC layers. Moreover, preparing coatings on the surface of SCS-6 fibers could help reduce the stress concentration on the fiber surface, increasing the fiber strength [113]. For W-core SiC fibers, severe interfacial reactions occurred between the W-core and SiC layer, generating brittle silicides and carbides of tungsten [103]. The interfacial reaction in W-core SiC fibers was very complex, and the interfacial reaction layer was the interface-dominated failure point, affecting the strength of the fiber [106]. In contrast, no interfacial reaction occurred between the C core and the SiC layer in the C-core SiC fibers, and the brittle C core was the internal initiation point of the cracks in the fibers. The morphology of the fiber surface is also an important factor. The inner tensile stress in the fiber surface makes the fiber sensitive to surface damage that occurs under stress or during manufacturing, further influencing the fiber’s strength. Thus, the smoother the surface of the SiC fibers is, the smaller the tensile stress distribution and the better the performance of the fibers. Luo *et al*. [106] found that the carbon coating on the outermost layer of SiC fibers could reduce the stress concentration on the surface of SiC fibers and nearly doubled the strength of the fibers compared with that without carbon coating, as shown in Fig. 4.

*

Reproduced with permission from Ref. [106], © Elsevier B.V. 2015.

When fabricating large-diameter SiC fibers via CVD, Zhang et al*. [91] controlled the deposition temperature of the CVD reactor to obtain two kinds of SiC fibers with W cores. The deposition temperatures of the obtained SiC-3200 and SiC-3800 fibers were 1200 and 1300 °C, respectively. The tensile strengths of the SiC-3200 and SiC-3800 fibers were 3.16 and 3.84 GPa, respectively. The two kinds of fibers possessed the same structure, with a fiber diameter of 108 µm, a SiC layer of 45 µm, and a carbon coating of 1.5 µm. Compared with the SiC-3200 fibers, the SiC-3800 fibers had fewer internal layer dislocations and coarser step fracture morphologies of the highly crystalline SiC columnar grains, resulting in higher tensile strengths. Liu *et al*. [107] fabricated SiC fibers by varying the input power in a CVD reactor, as shown in Fig. 5. When the input power increased from 300 to 360 W, the tensile strength of the SiC fibers increased from 1.57 to 3.03 GPa, and the Weibull modulus increased from 4.07 to 14.4 GPa. The morphology of the deposited SiC layer also changed from loosely packed particles to a tightly connected faceted structure, while the SiC grain size increased from 25 to 420 nm. The fast deposition rate, and the coarse and complete grains were the reasons for the improved fiber performance. However, when the input power increased to 380 W, the transition growth of the W/SiC interfacial region in the SiC fibers led to a decrease in the fiber tensile properties, consistent with the prediction of the critical interfacial layer thickness theory [119]. Moreover, Liu *et al*. [90] investigated the components (H2 and Ar) and flow rates of the reaction gases in a CVD reactor. Adding extra H2 increased the gas phase temperature and H2 concentration during deposition, reducing the formation of amorphous phases and structural defects (e.g., stacking defects and twins) during SiC growth and improving the fibers’ mechanical properties. In contrast, adding Ar could produce steep thermal gradients and lower the input power density in the CVD reactor, improving the fibers’ surface quality and the deposits’ homogeneity.

Hence, the core materials, surface coating, reaction temperature, input power, and reaction atmosphere greatly affect the performance of large-diameter SiC fibers. When applied as reinforcement, large-diameter SiC fibers further influence the mechanical properties of the whole composite materials in an actual application environment [55,85,87,120].

#### 2.1.2 Fine-diameter SiC fibers

**PD method**. Since Yajima *et al*. [121,122] employed the PD method to prepare SiC fibers, this method has been widely applied for preparing SiC fibers and other fibers [36,123,124]. Using the PD method, SiC fibers with a fine diameter (Fig. 6). Firstly, a polymer containing Si and C is synthesized as the precursor, which is melt-spun to form polymeric green fibers. Then, the green fibers are cured by air or electron beam to avoid fusing during the subsequent heating process. Finally, the cured fibers are pyrolyzed at high temperature to form SiC fibers [32]. The β-SiC grain size, free carbon content, and presence of pore defects affect the overall performance of SiC fibers [32,46,125,126]. According to the differences in elemental content, SiC fibers fabricated by the PD method are usually categorized into three generations. However, PD-SiC fibers can be divided more precisely into four types in terms of both their elemental composition and microstructure. The first type SiC fibers possess excess carbon (C/Si ≈ 1.3) and a high oxygen content (~12 wt%) with an amorphous microstructure. The second type SiC fibers have excess carbon (C/Si ≈ 1.3) and a low oxygen content (~1.0 wt%), exhibiting a crystalline microstructure with a grain size of ~5 nm. The third type SiC fibers have a near-stoichiometric composition (C/Si ≈ 1.0) with a low oxygen content of ~0.8 wt%, which still exhibit a crystalline microstructure with a grain size of ~10 nm. However, the fourth type SiC fibers have a near-stoichiometric composition (C/Si ≈ 1.0) with very low oxygen content (6,32].

*
Fig. 6Schematic digram of fabrication of ceramic fibers via PD route. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [32], © Wiley-VCH Verlag GmbH & Co. KGaA, Weinheim 2014.

Table 3 lists the fine-diameter SiC fibers and their properties. Currently produced SiC fibers exhibit tensile strength of up to 3 GPa and Young’s modulus of up to 400 GPa. Commercial SiC fibers include the following series: the Nicalon and Tyranno series from Japan, the Sylramic series from the United States, and the KD and Cansas series from China. Each series exhibits distinct characteristics due to variations in precursor materials and fabrication processes. The Nicalon series is produced by Nippon Carbon Co., Ltd., Japan, by utilizing polycarbosilane (PCS) as the precursor. The Tyranno series is produced by UBE Corporation, Japan, which introduces heterogeneous elements such as Zr, Ti, and Al into the PCS. Similarly, Dow Corning Company, USA, incorporates Ti into PCS and introduces B into the Sylramic fibers. The National University of Defense Technology (NUDT), China, synthesizes PCS precursor under atmospheric pressure for KD-II and KD-S fibers. Additionally, NUDT fabricates KD-SA fibers by using aluminum-containing PCS. Xiamen University in China has employed PCS as a precursor to produce Cansas-II fibers and Cansas-III fibers. The performance of SiC fibers in China is comparable to that of SiC fibers produced in Japan and the USA, as evidenced by Table 3.

The common first type SiC fibers are Nicalon, Tyranno Lox-M, and KD-I fibers. [34,127−129,157]. Nicalon fibers were fabricated mainly by curing the polymer fibers in air at 190 °C, followed by pyrolysis under an N2 atmosphere at 1200 °C [127]. UBE Corporation prepared polytitanocarbosilane (PTCS) by reacting tetrabutyl titanate (TBT) with PCS. Then, the PTCS fibers were treated by air curing and pyrolysis at 1200 °C to obtain Tyranno Lox-M fibers [128]. KD-I fibers were fabricated by air-curing PCS fibers and further heat treatment at 1300 °C [129,157]. The first type SiC fibers exhibited poor thermal stability above 1200 °C. The limited performance originated from an amorphous SiCx*O*y* phase due to the excess free carbon and high oxygen content (12 wt%) in the fibers. Upon heating, high temperature causes decomposition of the amorphous SiC*x*O*y*, leading to a sharp decrease in the tensile strength of the first type SiC fibers [157,158].

The second type SiC fibers include Hi-Nicalon [130−133], Tyranno Lox-E [135,159], Tyranno ZMI [134,136], KD-II [137,138], and Cansas-Ⅱ fibers [139,140]. To improve the high-temperature stability of SiC fibers, Nippon Carbon Co., Ltd. employed electron beam radiation instead of air to cure the PCS fibers. This modification prevented the introduction of extra oxygen into the fibers and improved the high-temperature stability of the fibers [160−162]. Additionally, UBE Corporation cured PTCS fibers by electron beam radiation to fabricate Tyranno Lox-E fibers. However, the oxygen content in the fibers still reached 5 wt%, and no significant improvement was observed in the fibers’ mechanical properties [32]. Then, this company introduced Zr into PCS to synthesize polyzirconocarbosilane (PZCS), followed by air curing and pyrolysis (N2, 1300 °C) to obtain Tyranno ZMI fibers [134,163]. In addition, electron beam radiation was utilized to cure the PZCS fibers, and Tyranno ZE fibers with a lower oxygen content were obtained [134,164]. Similarly, KD-II fibers with an oxygen content of 0.9 wt% were fabricated from precursor PCS, which showed a strength retention of more than 80% after annealing at 1400 °C for 1 h in an Ar atmosphere [137]. Additionally, Xiamen University developed Cansas-II SiC fibers [139,140,144−147,165,166], which exhibited a high tensile strength of 2.8 GPa and Young’s modulus of 270 GPa [139,140]. Compared with the first type SiC fibers, the second type SiC fibers had less oxygen, larger SiC grain sizes, and better high-temperature resistance [160].

The third type SiC fibers possess a near-stoichiometric composition with a crystalline microstructure [6,143,167,168], which is represented by Hi-Nicalon S [133,141,169,170], KD-S [6], and Cansas-III fibers [144−147]. To fabricate Hi-Nicalon S fibers [133,171], H2 was employed during the pyrolysis of the cured fibers, resulting in SiC fibers with a nearly stoichiometric ratio of C/Si (1.05). The fibers exhibited good mechanical properties, with tensile strength and Young’s modulus of 2.6 and 340 GPa, respectively [141,171]. Additionally, KD-S fibers were fabricated by curing through electron beam irradiation and pyrolyzing under a hydrogen-containing atmosphere. The tensile strength and Young’s modulus of the fibers were 2.6 and 320 GPa, respectively [142]. Similarly, decarburization by hydrogen was realized to fabricate Cansas-III fibers with a C/Si atomic ratio of 1.05, which had a tensile strength of 2.8 GPa and an elastic modulus of 310 GPa [144−147].

*
Fig. 7(a) Heat resistance of SiC fibers (after heat treatment in Ar for 1 h); (b) high-temperature creep resistance of SiC fibers. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [6], © Elsevier 2019.

With a near-stoichiometric composition and highly crystalline microstructure, the fourth type SiC fibers exhibit excellent high-temperature resistance, oxidation resistance, and creep resistance, represented by Tyranno SA [148−151], Sylramic [152−154,172,173], KD-SA [6,155,156], and KD ST fibers. For Tyranno SA fibers [148−151], Al was introduced to prepare polyaluminocarbosilane (PACS) fibers, and the PACS fibers were then cured by air and subjected to pyrolysis to obtain nearly stoichiometric polycrystalline SiC fibers (Fig. 8) [150]. The tensile strength and elastic modulus of Tyranno SA fibers were 2.4 and 375 GPa, respectively [174]. The Al promoted the crosslinking of the precursor polymer and increased the ceramic yield, which acted as a sintering aid to promote the densification of the fibers during sintering [175]. Recently, Tyranno SA4 fibers were produced with a diameter of 10 µm (Fig. 9) [151,176,177], for which the tensile strength was improved to 3.08 GPa [151]. For Sylramic fibers [174], Dow Corning Company introduced Ti and B into the fibers, which exhibited a tensile strength of 3.2 GPa and an elastic modulus of 400 GPa [152−154,178−180]. Then, the Sylramic-iBN fiber was fabricated by forming a boron nitride (BN) layer on the fiber surface through additional thermal treatment, resulting in improved oxidation resistance and increased thermal conductivity [172,181].

For the KD-SA fibers from the National University of Defense Technology (NUDT) [47,182,183], the PACS precursor was obtained by using low-softening-point polycarbosilane (LPCS) and aluminum acetylacetonate (Al(AcAc)3). Subsequently, Si–C–Al–O fibers were prepared by melt spinning, air curing, and pyrolysis, which were then sintered and densified at 1900 °C to obtain polycrystalline SiC fibers with a C/Si atomic ratio of 1.05 (Fig. 10(a)). The scanning electron microscopy (SEM) images of the obtained fibers are shown in Figs. 10(b) and 10(c). The tensile strength and elastic modulus of the fibers were 2.5 and 380 GPa, respectively. The fibers maintained their original tensile strength after annealing at 1800 °C for 5 h or at 1900 °C for 1 h under an inert atmosphere. Additionally, these fibers exhibited high strength retention (97%) after exposure to air at 1300 °C for 1 h. Moreover, under heat treatment at 1200–1500 °C for 1 h, the stress relaxation parameter m* of the fibers approximated that of Tyranno SA fibers. As shown in Fig. 11, Zhang *et al*. [184,185] synthesized PTCS using LPCS and TBT. Then, Si–C–Ti–B fibers, named as KD-ST fibers, were fabricated through melt spinning, curing, pyrolysis, and high-temperature sintering. The tensile strength of the fibers was measured to be 1.93 GPa. Moreover, the strength retention of the KD-ST fibers was 79.8% after 1 h of heat treatment in an Ar atmosphere at 2000 °C.

Overall, from the first type to the fourth type SiC fibers, their microstructures evolve from small β-SiC grains (x*O*y* phase to large β-SiC grains (> 100 nm) forming a dense structure in the fibers [186]. Figure 12 summarizes the microstructure of the four types of SiC fibers obtained via the PD method.

*
Fig. 12Schematic diagram of four types of SiC fibers fabricated by PD method.

In addition to aforementioned SiC fibers, the exploration of various high-performance SiC fibers has continued. Much effort has been devoted to modifying the preparation process of SiC fibers to enhance their comprehensive performance. Reducing the fiber diameter is an effective way to fabricate high-performance SiC fibers. Reducing the fiber diameter may facilitate degassing by diffusion and thus inhibit void formation [45]. Hence, controlling the ceramic diameter and defects is essential for achieving theoretical strength [46]. Refining the fiber diameter can effectively promote the sintering densification of SiC fibers and enhance the fibers’ mechanical properties. Kang et al*. [48] prepared SiC fibers with different fiber diameters (60, 14, and 9 µm) under the same conditions. When the SiC fiber diameter was refined from 60 to 9 µm, the fibers’ tensile strength increased from 0.28 to 1.52 GPa. In addition, for large-diameter SiC fibers (60 µm), the incomplete decomposition of SiC*x*O*y* in the fiber core resulted in excessive carbon enrichment at the boundaries of the β-SiC grains, preventing the growth and densification of the β-SiC particles during sintering. Furthermore, they fabricated ultrafine SiC fibers using PACS as the precursor via the centrifugal spinning technique [182,187,188], which possessed good flexibility and high-temperature stability at 2000 °C. They applied PACS as the precursor to fabricate flexible ultrafine nearly stoichiometric polycrystalline SiC fibers by combining electrospinning and the PD method (Fig. 13(a)) [37]. After heat treatment in Ar for 1 h, the strength retention of the ultrafine SiC fibers was 90.0% at 1800 °C, 94.2% at 1900 °C, and 86.4% at 2000 °C. The ultrafine SiC fibers showed no significant damage when being heated under a butane torch flame in the air and still exhibited excellent flexibility (Fig. 13(b)).

*
Fig. 13(a) Schematic diagram of fabricating ultrafine SiC fibers by electrospinning; (b) bending test of ultrafine SiC fibers under a flame of butane torch, and photos of ultrafine SiC fibers before and after butane torch treatment. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [37], © Elsevier Ltd. 2021.

Furthermore, the oxygen content and coating effects on the SiC fibers were investigated. Wang et al*. [189] controlled the oxygen content strictly during the fiber curing process and fabricated polycrystalline SiC fibers with a near stoichiometric ratio of C/Si (1.05). The obtained fibers had a tensile strength and elastic modulus of 2.16 and 406 GPa, respectively. After 1 and 10 h of exposure to an Ar atmosphere at 1800 °C, the strength retention of the fibers reached 91% and 61%, respectively. However, when the oxygen content was excessive (> 15 wt%), the fibers formed a skin–core structure, which was detrimental to the tensile strength. Zhang *et al*. [183] systematically investigated the microscopic morphology, composition, and structural evolution of SiC fibers at different temperatures (1600–2400 °C) and under different atmospheres (vacuum and Ar atmosphere). Under vacuum at 1600 °C or under Ar at 2000 °C, SiC grains decomposed and formed graphite due to Si sublimation. The transformation from SiC fibers to pure graphene fibers was completed at 1800 °C for 5 h or at 2000 °C for 1 h under vacuum, as shown in Fig. 14 [190]. After heat treatment under vacuum at 1800 °C for 1 h, the tensile strength retention of the SiC fibers with and without the PyC coating was 43.2% and 27.3%, respectively [191]. This difference could be attributed to the fact that the PyC coating on the surface of the SiC fibers could retard the Si sublimation of the SiC fibers at high temperature [183].

*
Fig. 14(a) Cross-sectional SEM image of SiC fiber; (b) cross-sectional SEM image, (c, d) TEM images; and (e) HRTEM image of obtained graphene fiber. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [190], © Elsevier Ltd. 2021.

Different curing methods and precursors have also been adopted to explore high-performance SiC fibers. Joo and Cho et al*. [36,192−195] worked on preparing SiC fibers via the PD approach using the iodine curing method. The SiC fibers had a tensile strength of 2.1 GPa [193]. The fibers were further treated at high temperatures (1600 and 1800 °C) to control the impurities and obtain polycrystalline SiC fibers [36]. Yao *et al*. [196] synthesized vinyl-modified polycarbosilane (Vi-PCS) by reacting PCS with divinyldimethylsilane (DMS) at 320 °C (Fig. 15(a)). Unmelted fibers were obtained by heating the electrospun ultrafine Vi-PCS fibers to 320 °C in an inert atmosphere. After pyrolysis at 1200 °C, the fibers were transformed into ultrafine SiC fibers with a uniform diameter (1.6–2.5 μm) and a low oxygen content of 4.64 wt%. The ultrafine SiC fibers retained a smooth surface and good flexibility after being treated with Ar at 1700 °C for 1 h, as shown in Fig. 15(b).

*
Fig. 15(a) Scheme of hydrosilylation reaction between PCS and DVS; (b) SEM images of ultrafine SiC fibers containing a low amount of oxygen before and after heat treatment (1700 °C, Ar, 1 h). Reproduced with permission from Ref. [196], © Elsevier Ltd and Techna Group S.r.l. 2020.

In summary, fine-diameter SiC fibers were successfully prepared using the PD method. Introducing suitable amounts of heterogeneous elements (Al, Ti, etc.), reducing the fiber diameter, and controlling excess C and O contents in the fibers during fabrication can influence the mechanical properties of SiC fibers. For future research, improving the tensile strength of highly crystalline SiC fibers with a nearly stoichiometric ratio is the main path for obtaining high-performance SiC fibers. Furthermore, reducing the cost of preparing SiC fibers is urgent [197]. For example, Li et al*. [198] used industrial silicon resin as a precursor to prepare SiCO fibers with an average tensile strength of 0.8 GPa and an elastic modulus of 100 GPa. This study provided a feasible method for preparing SiC fibers by using other low-cost silicon-containing compounds.

**Activated carbon fiber (ACF) carbothermal reduction (CR) method**. For the CR method, SiC fibers are obtained by reacting carbon fibers with SiO gas, as shown by Reaction (1):

(1) SiO(g)+2C(s)→SiC(s)+CO(g)

In 1994, Okada *et al*. [199,200] studied the reaction between ACF and silicon monoxide (SiO) at high temperature and low pressure to obtain SiC fibers, after which the fibers were densified by heat treatment. Since then, many researchers [201−210] have conducted related studies inspired by Okada’s work. For example, Ryu *et al*. [201] prepared SiC fibers by a vapor–solid (V–S) reaction between polyacrylonitrile-based activated carbon fibers (PAN-ACFs) and SiO gas. SiO gas was produced from a mixture of silicon (Si) and silicon dioxide (SiO2) at temperatures ranging from 1200 to 1300 °C in an inert atmosphere. In recent years, Frolova e*t al*. [202] explored the selection of a SiO gas source and designed a reactor for preparing SiC fibers using PAN-based carbon fibers. The tensile strength and elastic modulus of the obtained fibers were approximately 1.3 and 100 GPa, respectively. Additionally, C/SiC core–shell fibers were obtained due to the incomplete conversion of the carbon fibers (Fig. 16). The core–shell fibers had a high elastic modulus (370 GPa), which was much greater than that of the starting carbon fiber (230 GPa) [203]. However, when the carbon fibers were further silicified, the fibers underwent thermal expansion, producing significant cracks [204,205].

*
Fig. 16(a) SEM image of fiber tow after silicification treatment; (b) cross-sectional SEM image with EDX concentration profile of silicon. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [203], © Elsevier Ltd and Techna Group S.r.l. 2021.

Other substrate fibers have also been adopted and investigated besides using PAN-based carbon fibers. For example, Lee [206] explored different carbon fibers as substrates for preparing SiC fibers via the CR method and compared the detailed changes in PAN-based carbon fibers with those in pitch-based carbon fibers. The microstructure of the obtained SiC fibers depended on the carbon substrate materials under the same heat treatment conditions (Ar, 1500 °C, 4 h). In detail, the SiC fibers from the PAN-based carbon fibers consisted of 1 mm particles, while the particle size of the SiC fibers from the pitch-based carbon fibers was only 100 nm. Li et al*. [207] used carbonized skimmed cotton at high temperature to form carbon fibers and applied acid-leached coal gangue as the Si and carbon source. The SiO2 in the gangue reacted with carbon to form gaseous SiO, which diffused to the surface of the carbon fibers to form SiC fibers. Applying affordable leached gangue and skimmed cotton as the raw materials greatly reduced the cost of preparing SiC fibers.

#### 2.1.3 SiC nanofibers

Reducing the diameter of SiC fibers to the nanoscale can significantly alter their optical, electromagnetic, and other properties [211−213]. SiC nanofibers can be applied not only for high-temperature structural materials but also for sensors, supercapacitors, and catalyst materials [214−217]. Adding SiC nanofibers to CMC or MMC can significantly enhance the comprehensive performance of composites [50,218−220]. Electrospinning is the primary technology employed for fabricating SiC nanofibers [54,211,221−227]. Nanofiber preparation involves four key steps: precursor synthesis, electrospinning, curing treatment, and pyrolysis. The difference in the precursor solution directly affects the preparation and properties of the subsequent fibers. According to differences in the precursor solution, the fabrication methods for SiC nanofibers can be divided into two main types: PD method and sol–gel method. For the fabrication of SiC nanofibers using the PD method, PCS or other heterogeneous elements containing PCS are commonly employed in the precursor solution for spinning and subsequent heat treatment to produce SiC nanofibers. The sol–gel method is an alternative approach in which silicon-containing compounds and carbon sources are combined in the precursor solution. This solution undergoes hydrolysis and condensation, which is then transformed into fibers by electrospinning. Subsequently, SiC nanofibers are generated through CR between SiO2 and C within the fibers at elevated temperatures.

Many researchers have prepared SiC nanofibers based on the PD method [54,211,221−228]. Hu *et al*. [221] fabricated PCS fibers with a diameter of 1 µm by electrospinning using PCS as the precursor. Then, the PCS fibers were transformed into SiC nanofibers (800 nm) by curing treatment and pyrolysis (N2, 1200 °C, 2 h). Similarly, An *et al*. [222] fabricated SiC nanofibers by electrospinning and carefully studied the effect of the electrospinning parameters and the content of PCS and polyvinylpyrrolidone (PVP) in the spinning solution on the diameter of the fibers. When the PCS content increased from 5 to 10 wt% (PVP content was 5 wt%), the diameter of the nanofibers increased from a few hundred nanometers to near 2 μm. The reason was that increasing the PCS content led to the intersection and entanglement of molecular chains. When the PCS content was fixed at 5 wt%, the diameter of the SiC fibers increased as the PVP content increased. This was because of the solution viscosity’s significant effect on the fiber diameter. A high viscosity increased the relaxation time of the solution and hindered jet elongation, resulting in a larger fiber diameter. Therefore, PCS and PVP concentrations of 5 wt% were chosen to prepare the precursor solution. After electrospinning, curing treatment, and high-temperature pyrolysis (N2, 1300 °C, 3 h), SiC nanofibers with a diameter of 600±50 nm were obtained. Similarly, Wang *et al*. [211] used PCS as the precursor and added PVP and dimethylformamide (DMF) as spinning aids for electrospinning. SiC nanofibers with different fiber diameters (200 nm–1 µm) were obtained by adjusting the mass ratio of PCS in the precursor solution. When the mass ratio of PCS was 10 wt%, the obtained SiC nanofibers exhibited excellent electromagnetic wave absorption performance. Figure 17 shows a schematic diagram of fabricating SiC nanofibers by electrospinning. On the other hand, some researchers have attempted to fabricate SiC nanofibers by the sol–gel method. Li *et al*. [229] prepared a gel-like mixture using ethyl orthosilicate (TEOS) and PVP, which were subsequently hydrolyzed and aged, followed by the preparation of green fibers through electrospinning. Upon exposure to high temperature, SiO2 underwent a reaction with C, resulting in the formation of SiC in the fibers. However, under elevated temperature conditions, the fibers exhibited severe shrinkage, leading to the structural collapse of the SiC nanowires. Similarly, the collapse of SiC fibers prepared by the sol–gel method has also been reported in the literature [223,230,231]. This phenomenon was mainly due to the intense reaction between SiO2 and C inside the fibers to produce CO and CO2 gases, the release of which led to damage to the fiber shape.

Fig. 17Schematic diagram of process of fabricating SiC nanofibers by electrospinning. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [222], Elsevier Ltd. and Techna Group S.r.l. 2019.

Overall, the fabrication of SiC nanofibers using electrospinning has attracted significant attention. The SiC nanofibers fabricated from the PCS precursor show excellent mechanical properties, high-temperature resistance, and electromagnetic wave absorption. SiC nanofibers fabricated with silicon-containing compounds of low molecular weight as raw materials are prone to severe structural shrinkage and collapse of fiber shape after high-temperature treatment due to their high oxygen content.

### 2.2 Boron carbide (B<sub>4</sub>C) fibers

B4C has a low density (2.52 g·cm−3), high melting point (2450 °C), excellent chemical stability, and good neutron absorption interface (a high neutron absorption cross-section of 767 b for thermal neutrons). Its hardness reaches 37 GPa, which is lower than industrial diamonds but higher than silicon carbide ceramic materials [64−67]. Research on B4C has focused mainly on its application as a wear-resistant material, such as B4C powder and bulk ceramics [64,67,232−236]. Applying fibers instead of nanoparticles as reinforcing materials could effectively prevent agglomeration and maintain the strength of the composites [237]. Compared with B4C particle-reinforced composites, B4C fiber-reinforced composites showed greater mechanical strength [238].

Reports on preparing B4C ceramic fibers have gradually increased in recent years [238−242]. Pender *et al*. [239,240] used porous alumina (Al2O3) membranes as templates to prepare B4C nanofibers (Fig. 18). They immersed the Al2O3 membranes in a homemade solution of boron-containing polymer precursor and then pyrolyzed the templates at 1025 °C to obtain B4C fiber-filled membranes. Finally, the Al2O3 membranes were dissolved in a hydrofluoric acid solution, releasing the B4C fibers. The obtained B4C fibers had a diameter of approximately 250 nm and a length of approximately 45 µm. The fibers, mainly composed of crystalline B4C, were homogeneous and highly aligned. Furthermore, the fiber morphology could be changed by controlling the solution concentration and the number of membrane treatments, and B4C hollow fibers were obtained.

*
Fig. 18(a) SEM image of B4C nanofibers; (b) TEM image showing open end of a cylindrical B4C nanostructure. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [240], © American Chemical Society 2000.

Kakiage and Kobayashi [242] attempted to prepare B4C fibers by combining sol–gel and electrospinning. H3BO3/PVA solution with a molar ratio of 1 : 4 was added to glycerol as a plasticizer, and H3BO3/PVA precursor fibers were fabricated by electrospinning. The obtained precursor fibers were subjected to heat treatment (640 °C, air, 2 h) and thermal decomposition (1400 °C, Ar, 5 h) to obtain B4C fibers consisting of connected submicron-sized particles. It was further investigated that extending the heat treatment time promoted particle grain growth, increasing the fiber particle size. Kesici and Kiraz [238] added ethanol (C2H5OH) and cetyltrimethylammonium bromide (CTAB) to a mixture of H3BO3 and PVP and then prepared B4C nanofibers by electrospinning at low temperature. In addition, to improve the properties of the B4C nanofibers, [3-(methacryloxy)propyl]trimethoxysilane (MPTS) was used so that the fibers could be chemically bound to the organic matrix and uniformly dispersed in the matrix.

Additionally, B4C fibers were prepared using a mixture of H3BO3, the boron source, followed by electrospinning and CR reactions. B4C was obtained by a carbon thermal reduction (Reaction (2) [242]:

(2) 7C+2B2O3→B4C+6CO

Shawgi et al*. [241] varied the molar ratio of C/B2O3 from 3.1 to 2.9 while preparing B4C fibers to control the amount of carbon. While preparing B4C fibers, Kakiage and Kobayashi [242] set the C/B2O3 molar ratio to 3.2, which slightly deviated from the stoichiometric ratio of C/B2O3.

## 3 Transition metal carbide ceramic fibers

Transition-metal carbide ceramic materials with melting points above 3000 °C are typically regarded as UHTCs [69,243]. Figure 19 shows that the melting points of transition metal carbides are greater than those of other materials, such as pure metals, borides, and nitrides [56]. These UHTCs possess high melting points, hardnesses, and strengths, indicating their great potential for application under extreme conditions [3,56,244]. However, these UHTCs have disadvantages, such as poor fracture toughness and poor thermal shock resistance [69]. Additionally, difficulties emerge in processing these UHTCs due to their high hardness [245].

![](https://wqketang.oss-cn-beijing.aliyuncs.com/zip-unzip/zip-c0c3641f-a96b-4598-afce-af9e73d471eb/JAC0936/files/JAC0936-19.jpg?Expires=1766505781&OSSAccessKeyId=STS.NZaSWrpFP6nuZWQLxNiDpx1od&Signature=Ya%2FpT%2BpIlamESwgbPBGPGm0DnHE%3D&x-oss-process=image%2Fresize%2Cm_fixed%2Cw_600&security-token=CAISywJ1q6Ft5B2yfSjIr5nUGO3GnZlxgayeWHHgqHgbZctcl%2FTEhjz2IHtKenhsBOsbtfk1mG5W5%2FgZlqJ9SptIAEfJa9d99MzfUJQhv9GT1fau5Jko1beHewHKeTOZsebWZ%2BLmNqC%2FHt6md1HDkAJq3LL%2Bbk%2FMdle5MJqP%2B%2FUFB5ZtKWveVzddA8pMLQZPsdITMWCrVcygKRn3mGHdfiEK00he8TouufTinpHMskGA1Aell7Mvyt6vcsT%2BXa5FJ4xiVtq55utye5fa3TRYgxowr%2Fwo0v0YpGya5YzHXwcPskvdKZbo78UqLQlla6w%2BGqFJqvPxr%2Fp8t%2Fx5fWJKAezhVgs8cVM8JOjIqKOscIsiJk4nzku%2BlV55c8Fdm%2BgUooJVgIMhTnduUfAPJAGOxzJitP%2BUVGGphr60TEnBL4rB5MUctfzRp2YQC8fUTzDnGoABTY0XsvWdY8LzFDdA%2F2hwFlLwSRnnzSYCvte8TKTahxt%2Bvolu05PooxBld5O2ctlrxI1rexU84fFrgLDzOFU73uwEa2TCBsaKEtzEihiPzmOCrbZyN44dNvlQPKfzOsbjfsPJ0Y%2Bjc%2BbaOPbxmj8V7bmqGmH4vX9JLuiJJ26Vbd0gAA%3D%3D)
Fig. 19Melting points of several pure metals, transition metal carbides, oxides, borides, and nitrides. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [56], © Acta Materialia Inc. 2016.

Numerous studies have reported the synthesis of powders, nanowires, and bulk materials of transition metal carbide ceramics [246−251], but few studies have investigated transition metal carbide ceramic fibers and their application [252]. To date, the fabrication of transition-metal carbide ceramic fibers has focused mainly on MeC fibers (Me = Zr, Ti, Hf, and Ta) and their complex-phase ceramic fibers [31,253−255]. Similarly, transition metal carbide ceramic fibers can be used as reinforcements in composites to enhance their flexural strength and fracture toughness [69].

There are two types of fabrication methods for transition metal carbide ceramic fibers. One is to synthesize a precursor solution containing metal and C and then prepare transition metal carbide ceramic fibers by sol–gel method. The other is fabricating transition metal carbide ceramic fibers using the PD method. Table 4 lists the works on fabricating transition metal carbide ceramic fibers. As shown in Table 4, most single-phase transition metal carbide ceramic fibers are fabricated using different spinning techniques combined with PD/sol–gel methods. Metal salts/metal alcohol salts are mixed into the precursor solution, and spinning additives are added to prepare the spinning solution. Transition metal carbide ceramic fibers are fabricated by spinning, curing, and high-temperature thermal decomposition. The spinning additives not only improve the spinnability of the precursor solution but also provide enough carbon content when the precursor fiber undergoes a CR reaction.

### 3.1 Titanium carbide (TiC) fibers

The crystal structure of TiC is a NaCl-type structure. Ti atoms with a larger atomic radius form a face-centered cubic (FCC) structure, and C atoms with a smaller atomic radius occupy octahedral pores. The crystal structure of TiC determines its unique properties, such as a high melting point (3067 °C), high hardness (38.35 GPa), low density (4.91 g·cm−3), excellent wear resistance, good electrical conductivity, and chemical stability [3,20,31,65,68]. Apart from serving as lightweight structural materials, TiC fibers can be used for electrochemistry, such as oxygen reduction reaction (ORR) catalysts and catalyst carriers in fuel cells [273], supercapacitor electrode materials [274], and porous agents [275−277]. Researchers [259,267−269,278] have synthesized polymer precursors containing Ti or used the sol–gel method to synthesize compounds containing Ti and C as precursors to fabricate TiC fibers.

Yu *et al*. [269] used poly(tetrabutyl titanate) (TBOT) and Haac to synthesize a polysulfoxane (PTO) solution as the titanium source while choosing PVP as the spinning aid and carbon source. PTO and PVP solutions were mixed to prepare the precursor solution (Fig. 20(a)). Then, electrospinning was adopted to fabricate precursor fibers with a diameter of 1.22 µm. Finally, the obtained precursor fibers were further transformed into continuous TiC fibers (550 nm) by heating at 1500 °C for 1 h under an Ar atmosphere. As shown in Fig. 20(b), by increasing the heat treatment temperature from 1200 to 1600 °C, some changes in the diameter and morphology of the TiC fibers occurred. The formation route of the TiC fibers is shown by Reaction (3):

*
Fig. 20(a) Synthesis route of PTO solution and Ti-containing precursor; (b) SEM images of precursor fibers heated at 250 °C and TiC fibers prepared at different temperatures (1200–1600 °C). Reproduced with permission from Ref. [269], © Elsevier Ltd and Techna Group S.r.l. 2020.

(3) Precursorfiber→TiO2+C→TiO2+TiCxOy+C→TiCxOy+C→TiCfiber

During this process, the phase composition of the fibers changed from titania (TiO2) and TiCx*O*y* (1200 and 1300 °C) to TiC*x*O*y* (1400 °C). Subsequently, TiC fibers with Ti/C of near stoichiometric ratio were obtained by removing O from TiC*x*O*y* during thermal decomposition at 1500 and 1600 °C. TiC nanocrystals with different sizes of 100–400 nm were randomly distributed in the fibers. However, the surface oxidation of the TiC fibers was very weak, although TiO2 and TiC*x*O*y* were generated after the fibers being exposed to air.

In contrast, Tsai and Mondal [267] reported a facile method for synthesizing TiC nanofibers from TiO2 fibers by modified carbon thermal reduction and electrospinning. They fabricated TiO2 fibers by electrospinning using TiP as the titanium source and PVP as the spinning aid. Then, the obtained TiO2 fibers were transformed into TiC nanofibers with smooth surfaces and a diameter of 148 nm by heat treatment (Ar, 1500 °C, 6 h) with cellulose paper as the applied carbon source. Furthermore, the morphology of the final product fibers and the phase composition transformation (from TiO2 to TiC*x*O*y* and TiC) in the fibers could be affected by the processing conditions, such as the addition of a carbon source to the Ti-containing fibers, carbon mixing, and heat treatment temperature. The phase transformation of the TiC nanofibers is shown in Reaction (4):

(4) TiO2(s)+3C(s)→TiC(s)+2CO(g)

Similarly, Khail *et al*. [268] used the sol–gel method to prepare a PAN/Ti composite solution by mixing TiP, N,N-dimethylformamide, and PAN. Then, TiO2/PAN precursor fibers were fabricated by electrostatic spinning and finally transformed into TiC nanofibers (90 nm) by heat treatment (Ar, 1000 °C, 3 h). Firbas *et al*. [259] used TiP as the titanium source but chose furfuryl alcohol (FA) and PVP as the carbon source and spinning aid, respectively. A high-viscosity TiO2 precursor solution was prepared by changing the process parameters in the sol–gel method. Then, the TiO2/C fibers were fabricated by spinning under centrifugal force and finally transformed into TiC fibers (10 µm) by heat treatment (Ar, 1450 °C, 5 h).

As mentioned above, TiC fibers have been prepared using various methods, which affect the fiber structure and properties. Currently, research on TiC fibers has focused mainly on fiber formation, and little research has been conducted on their properties. Therefore, it is important to study the preparation and properties of TiC fibers together to promote their application as lightweight structural materials.

### 3.2 Zirconium carbide (ZrC) fibers

ZrC has an ultrahigh melting point (3420 °C), high hardness (27 GPa), low density (6.73 g·m−3), good electrical conductivity, and high thermal conductivity (24.6 W·m−1·K−1), as well as good radiation resistance and oxidation resistance [20,31,69,70]. Thus, ZrC is often used as an ultrahigh temperature material [279]. Fibrillating ZrC is expected to yield new ceramic fibers for application at ultrahigh temperatures. Many studies on transition metal carbide ceramic fibers have been conducted on ZrC fibers. ZrC fibers are mainly fabricated using the PD/sol–gel method. Different spinning techniques are typically employed to fabricate ZrC fibers with various diameters. ZrC fibers with larger diameters (> 1 µm) are mainly fabricated by hand stretching, melt spinning, and centrifugal spinning (Fig. 21(a)) [256−259,280,281]. In contrast, ZrC nanofibers are fabricated by electrospinning (Fig. 21(b)) [70,123,260−266].

*
Fig. 21(a) Schematic diagram of centrifugal melt-spinning system for preparing ZrC precursor fibers. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [256], © Elsevier B.V. 2022. (b) Schematic diagram of preparation of Zr(OPr)4/PVP nanofibers by electrospinning. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [70], © The American Ceramic Society 2016.

For ZrC fibers with micron-sized diameters, Firbas et al*. [259] fabricated ZrO2 fibers by improved centrifugal spinning using n-propylated zirconium, FA, and PVP as the raw materials. Then, the ZrO2 fibers were heat treated (Ar, 1450 °C, 5 h) to obtain ZrC fibers. Chen *et al*. [258] applied zirconium acetate, sucrose, and PVP as zirconium sources, carbon sources, and spinning aids, respectively, and controlled the molar ratio of zirconium acetate to sucrose in the range of 4:1 to 4:1.5. Then, the precursor fibers were fabricated by centrifugal spinning and converted into ZrC fibers by heat treatment (Ar, 1600 °C, 2 h). The obtained ZrC fibers with smooth surfaces had diameters of 1–3 µm. When the sucrose content in the precursor solution was high, the residual carbon in the ZrC fibers allowed the fibers to maintain their morphology and high-temperature stability. In the fibers, the presence of residual carbon reduced the mutual contact of ZrC particles and prevented the coarsening of ZrC particles at high temperatures, thus improving the microstructural stability of the fibers at a high temperature of 2000 °C. Wang *et al*. [256] fabricated precursor fibers (29.2 µm) using the centrifugal-melt spinning technique based on zirconium tetrachloride (ZrCl4), acetylacetone (AcAc), methanol, and 1,4-butanediol as raw materials [257]. For the fabrication of ZrC fibers, as the heat treatment temperature increased from 1300 to 1500 °C, the conversion from the precursor polymer solution to ZrC involved three steps: the decomposition of organic groups, the formation of ZrO2, and the conversion to ZrC by carbon thermal reduction (Fig. 22(a)). The precursor fibers were shrunk by heat treatment (Ar, 1500 °C, 2 h) to obtain ZrC fibers with a diameter of 15.5 μm (Figs. 22(b) and 22(c)).

*
Fig. 22(a) XRD patterns of ZrC precursor fibers after pyrolysis at different temperatures (1300–1500 °C); (b, c) SEM images of ZrC fibers after pyrolysis at 1500 °C. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [256], © Elsevier B.V. 2022.

In contrast, ZrC fibers with a smaller fiber diameter (et al*. [264] used PZO as the zirconium source [265], PAN as the spinning aid and primary carbon source, and sucrose as an additional carbon source. The nanofibers (600 nm) composed of uniform ZrC nanocrystals were obtained by electrospinning and subsequent carbon thermal reduction (Ar, 1400 °C, 2 h). It was further investigated whether nanofibers with core–shell structures could be obtained only when PAN was applied as the carbon source. The shell particles were much larger than the core particles, indicating significant growth of the ZrO2 particles prior to the ceramic transformation. Apparently, the difference in the carbon source significantly affected the morphology of the ZrC fibers. Ghelich *et al*. [70] prepared an electrospinning solution by dissolving n-propanol zirconium (Zr(OPr)4) in glacial acetic acid and 2,4-pentanedione and then mixing it in PVP/ethanol solution to fabricate precursor fibers via electrostatic spinning. The precursor fibers were finally converted into ZrC nanofibers by a CR reaction (Ar, 1450 °C, 3 h). Song *et al*. [123] successfully prepared ZrC nanofibers from a precursor mixture by adding PAN/DMF to a toluene solution of ZrCl4, ethylenediamine (EDA), and diallylamine (DAA).

To fabricate ZrC nanofibers, Tao *et al*. [260] developed a novel polymer precursor called polyzirconosaal (PZSA) by combining PZO [261] with SA (Fig. 23(a)). Subsequently, PVP was introduced as a spinning aid to obtain PZSA/PVP fibers (400 nm) by electrospinning (Fig. 23(b)). The –OH groups of PZSA interacted with the C=O groups of PVP to form hydrogen bonds, thus improving the spinnability and stability of the precursor solution. Additionally, the C=O groups in PVP could coordinate metal atoms, thus making the metal atoms in the precursor fibers uniformly distributed [72]. The PZSA/PVP fibers were converted into ZrC nanofibers (200 nm) by heating at 1300 °C for 2 h under an Ar atmosphere. After the pyrolysis of the PZSA/PVP fibers, the morphology of the ZrC nanofibers was maintained. However, the fibers’ surface became rough due to removing organic components and ZrC crystallization.

### 3.3 Tantalum carbide (TaC) and hafnium carbide (HfC) fibers

Among UHTCs, HfC, and TaC have the highest known melting points, 3890 and 3880 °C, respectively. HfC and TaC ceramic materials have a wide range of applications in aerospace and other fields [282,283]. Current studies have focused on applying HfC and TaC ceramics as matrix materials [284−286]. Moreover, the preparation and application of HfC and TaC particles and whiskers have also been widely studied [43,44,74,287−289]. However, there are few reports on HfC and TaC fibers [270−272,290,291].

HfC and TaC fibers are mainly fabricated by sol–gel and PD methods combined with various spinning techniques. For example, Lee *et al*. [271] adjusted the viscosity of the PAN/DMF spinning solution and added ethanol tantalum(V) and tert-butanol hafnium(IV) to the PAN/DMF mixture. Then, the precursor fibers were fabricated by forced spinning and further transformed into TaC fibers (475 nm) and HfC fibers (340 nm). Both fibers maintained oxidation resistance and thermal stability in air upon heating to 450 °C. Nakane *et al*. [272] mixed cellulose acetate (CA) and tantalum pentoxide (Ta2O5) to fabricate CA-alcohol salt gel precursor fibers. Then, the melt spinning technique and high-temperature heat treatment (Ar, 1100–1500 °C, 1 h) were used to obtain TaC fibers with large diameters (0.3–1.0 mm). The formation process is shown in Reaction (5):

(5) Ta2O5(s)+7C(s)→2TaC(s)+5CO(g)

The oxygen and carbon required during the reaction were supplied by CA. Cheng *et al*. [270] used HfCl4 as the hafnium source to synthesize polymer precursors containing Hf–N and CH=CH groups (Fig. 24(a)). Then, the precursor fibers were obtained by melting and hand-stretching techniques. After thermal decomposition in Ar at 1600 °C for 1 h, the precursor fibers were converted into HfC fibers with a diameter of 13 µm (Figs. 24(b)–24(e)). However, the obtained HfC fibers were too brittle to measure their tensile strength.

## 4 Complex-phase carbide ceramic fibers

As mentioned above, SiC fibers have received much attention among carbide ceramic fibers. While fabricating high-performance SiC fibers is mainly based on PCS as precursor, it has been found that doping metal elements, such as Ti, Zr, Al, and B, can improve fiber properties, such as high-temperature resistance, thermal conductivity, and creep resistance [47,134,182,184]. Usually, compared with single-phase carbide ceramic fibers, complex-phase carbide ceramic fibers show superior overall performance. For example, HfC/SiC fibers could improve creep resistance and inhibit grain boundary sliding by dispersing HfC with a high self-diffusion coefficient in a SiC matrix (Fig. 25) [292]. Table 5 lists some raw materials, preparation methods, and applications of complex-phase carbide ceramic fibers. Many researchers have investigated composite ceramic fibers with binary systems, such as B4C/SiC [293], TiC/SiC [294], ZrC/SiC [295−297], and HfC/SiC fibers [292,298,299]. Furthermore, there are extensive studies on the preparation and properties of composite ceramic fibers with ternary systems, quaternary systems, and even more dopant elements in multiple systems. Examples include ZrCSiB fibers [253], SiBNO fibers [300,301], SiBOC fibers [254,302], SiCBN fibers [303,304], SiZrOC fibers [305−307], and SiZrNOC fibers [255]. In addition to these SiC-based ceramic fibers, reports can be found on preparing ZrC-based ceramic fibers, such as ZrC/ZrB2 fibers and polyurethane/ZrC fibers, for being applied as ultrahigh temperature-resistant reinforcing materials and photothermal conversion materials [308−312]. Moreover, carbon-based carbide ceramic fibers, such as TiC/C fibers [313−318], TaC/C fibers [319,320], and ZrC/C fibers [123], have been fabricated for application in catalysis, thermal insulation, and electrochemistry.

For SiC-based carbide ceramic fibers, Bae et al*. [292,298] prepared polymer precursors by adding aluminum and hafnium sources (hafnium(IV) acetylacetonate (Hf(C5H7O2)4)) to PCS as sintering aids and a second-phase dispersion enhancer, respectively. Then, HfC/SiC fibers with a diameter of 11 μm were fabricated by melt spinning and high-temperature sintering. After being sintered at 2000 °C, the fibers still possessed a smooth surface with no obvious pore defects in their cross-sections. The Hf atoms were uniformly distributed on the surface and throughout the interior of the fibers (Figs. 26(a) and 26(b)). Moreover, HfC, which has a high self-diffusion coefficient, was dispersed in the SiC matrix, increasing the creep resistance of the fibers and inhibiting grain boundary sliding. The stress relaxation parameters and apparent activation energy of the HfC/SiC fibers were greater than those of the commercial SiC fibers (Tyranno SA3 and Hi-Nicalon Type S) at high temperatures (Figs. 26(c) and 26(d)). Hou *et al*. [299] used hafnium acetylacetonate (Hf(AcAc)4) as a hafnium source to prepare a homogeneous PCS/Hf(AcAc)4 precursor solution. Then, HfC/SiC fibers with different Hf contents were obtained by combining electrostatic spinning and carbon thermal reduction. When the HfC content increased from 0 to 2.5 wt%, the average diameter of the fibers decreased from 2.62 μm to 260 nm. This decrease occurred because Hf(AcAc)4 in the PCS solution enhanced the electrical conductivity and spinnability of the solution, resulting in the thinning of the nanofibers. In addition, increasing the Hf content enhanced the flexibility of the HfC/SiC fibers, which have good microwave absorption properties.

Similarly, other metals (Ti, Zr, Fe, and Co) have been doped into SiC fibers to alter their properties and expand their application [297,323−325]. Joo et al*. [294] used TIP to crosslink with polycarbosilane to synthesize PTCS as the precursor. Then, TiC/SiC fibers with diameters of 3–8 μm were obtained by the PD method combined with electrospinning. Zhang *et al*. [296] prepared PZCS as an organic precursor [321,326] and fabricated ZrC/SiC fibers by precursor pretreatment, melt spinning, electron-beam curing treatment, and high-temperature pyrolysis. The obtained Zr/SiC fibers with a diameter of 12 µm had smooth surfaces and dense structures. The tensile strength and Young’s modulus of the ZrC/SiC fibers were 2.7 and 266 GPa, respectively. Similarly, Tian *et al*. [321] prepared PZCS from dimethyldichlorosilane (C2H6Cl2Si), ZrCl2, and sodium metal (Na) in toluene (Fig. 27(a)). Then, ZrC/SiC fibers (10 µm) were obtained from PZCS by heat treatment at 1400 °C, which showed a ZrC mass concentration of 15%. The grain size ranged from 5–30 nm with a uniform distribution of SiC and ZrC (Figs. 27(b)–27(e)).

*

Reproduced with permission from Ref. [321], © The Authors 2015.

For ZrC-based carbide ceramic fibers, Li et al*. [308] prepared a precursor solution of C12H28O4Zr2, H2BO3, sucrose, PVP, and DMF. Then, ZrC/ZrB2 nanofibers with a diameter of 100 nm were fabricated by combining electrospinning and carbon thermal reduction. The content of ZrC in the ZrC/ZrB2 nanofibers could be adjusted by changing the molar ratio of *n*(B)/*n*(Zr) in the precursor solution, and the addition of ZrC to ZrB2 could enhance the plasticity of the composites. When *n*(B)/*n*(Zr) was 3.5, very close to the eutectic composition of the ZrC/ZrB2 composites, the fabrication temperature of the ZrC/ZrB2 composites decreased, and the fracture toughness of ZrB2 improved. Similarly, Xu *et al*. [309,327] prepared a precursor solution using n-propanol, zirconium, sucrose, and boric acid as raw materials to fabricate ZrC/ZrB2 nanofibers with different contents of ZrC (Fig. 28). In addition, for photothermal conversion materials, Yan *et al*. [310] proposed photothermal polyurethane (PU)/ZrC fibers obtained by electrospinning. While testing the photothermal conversion performance of the fibers, the surface temperature of the fibers increased to 66.5 °C after irradiation by a xenon lamp for 150 s when the content of ZrC reached 8%. Hence, these fibers could compensate for heat loss in human bodies by absorbing and converting light energy.

In addition, some researchers have focused on preparing carbon-based composite ceramic fibers with carbides. Carbon-based ceramic fibers incorporating different carbides possess different performance advantages. Wei *et al*. [314] successfully fabricated TiC/C nanofibers with different TiC contents by electrostatic spinning. The diameter of the TiC/C nanofibers ranged from 80 to 200 nm, and the inner TiC particle size ranged from 20 to 30 nm. Adjusting the ratio of TiC/C could change the catalytic performance of the materials. Yu *et al*. [315] similarly fabricated TiC/C nanofibers (300 nm) and found that these nanofibers exhibited excellent electrochemical and structural stability as NH3 catalysts. Ren *et al*. [316] used TBT and PVP as raw materials and varied the composition of the reaction atmosphere (Ar and air) to fabricate TiC/C nanofibers (100 nm) with different TiC contents. The synergistic enhancement effect between TiC and C increased the specific capacitance of the TiC/C fibers with good electrochemical properties.

Guo *et al*. [319] prepared TaC/C fibers using a precursor solution of PAN containing TaCl5, followed by electrospinning and high-temperature pyrolysis (Fig. 29(a)). During the preoxidation and carbonization of the TaC/C fibers, the PAN in the fibers underwent dehydrogenation and cyclization reactions. As shown in Fig. 29(b), the PAN transformed from a step structure to a carbonized structure with decreasing N atoms as the temperature increased. The fibers’ diameters decreased when the pyrolysis temperature increased from 800 to 1400 °C (Fig. 29(c)). Moreover, when the content of TaCl5 was 40 wt%, the TaC/C fibers obtained at 1200 °C exhibited excellent flexibility and superior electromagnetic interference shielding properties [328]. Atchison *et al*. [252] used a sol–gel process to obtain TiO2/C, NbO/C, and ZrO/C nanofibers by blending carrier polymers (CA or PVP) with titanium butoxide (Ti(IV)BO), zirconium acetylacetonate, or *n*-butylniobium oxide and electrospinning. On this basis, thermal decomposition treatment at 1300–1700 °C was carried out to effectively transform metal oxide/carbon fibers into metal carbide/carbon nanofibers (TiC/C, NbC/C, and ZrC/C) while maintaining the integrity of the fibers.

The preparation methods used for the carbide ceramic nanofibers are presented in Tables 4 and 5. The electrospinning method is the principal technique for preparing carbide ceramic nanofibers. However, the properties of the resulting fibers are directly influenced by the precursor solutions used in their fabrication. SiC nanofibers fabricated with PCS as a precursor demonstrate exceptional mechanical properties, while those produced using polysiloxane or small molecule silicone-containing compounds (e.g., tetraethoxysilane) exhibit structural collapse at high temperature. Conversely, single-phase carbide ceramic nanofibers or complex-phase nanofibers containing high melting point transition metal carbides display excellent resistance to elevated temperatures.

## 5 Applications of carbide ceramic fibers

Currently, SiC fibers are the most widely used carbide ceramic fibers. Due to their high melting point, good mechanical properties, and high-temperature oxidation resistance, SiC fibers are often used as high-temperature-resistant structural materials in the aerospace and nuclear industries. In addition, SiC is also an ideal semiconductor material and can be used as a functional material for electrochemical applications. There are many reports on applying SiC fibers but few on other carbide ceramic fibers, especially transition metal carbide fibers with ultrahigh melting points. Moreover, the current status of research on carbide ceramic fibers has rarely been reported systematically. This paper systematically introduces the application of carbide ceramic fibers as high-temperature-resistant structural materials in the aerospace and nuclear industries, emphasizing SiC fibers. Moreover, the application of carbide ceramic fibers in catalyst carriers, sensors, and supercapacitors is briefly introduced.

### 5.1 High-temperature-resistant structural materials

The comprehensive performance of ceramic matrix composites (CMCs), MMCs, and UHTCs has been improved by using ceramic fibers as reinforcement [5,7,8]. These composites have greatly broadened their application and played an important role in the aerospace and nuclear industries [12,62]. SiC fibers are currently the most widely used carbide ceramic fibers. Large-diameter SiC fibers are used to reinforce MMCs, and fine-diameter SiC fibers are mainly used to reinforce CMCs and UHTCs [2,329−332]. SiC nanofiber-reinforced composites have also been reported [50,333]. Figure 30 shows the main application of SiC fibers, such as in aircraft engines and nuclear applications [329].

#### 5.1.1 CMCs

**Fundamentals of SiC****f****/SiC CMC**. Since the 1980s, researchers have attempted to apply monolithic ceramics as hot components of gas turbines because of their attractive thermal stability and high strength [334−338]. However, monolithic ceramics suffer from brittleness and poor thermal shock resistance and fail to satisfy the requirements of complex service environments. In contrast, ceramic fiber-reinforced ceramic composites have inherited the advantages of ceramics while significantly improving toughness and crack extension resistance, resulting in better durability and thermal shock resistance in their construction [339]. Especially in the last 30 years, fiber-reinforced CMCs, represented by SiCf/SiC, have undergone rapid development and breakthroughs in high-performance fiber manufacturing technology, composite densification technology, interface technology, and environmental barrier coating technology [340−343]. These breakthroughs have greatly improved the temperature resistance, antioxidant activity, and thermal shock resistance of SiCf/SiC CMCs.

SiCf/SiC CMCs consist of three components (Fig. 31): reinforcing fibers, a matrix, and an interface between the fibers and the matrix [2,329]. The reinforcing fibers serve as the bone, the matrix as the flesh of the composites, and the interface connects the bone and flesh. The reinforcing fibers not only function as one of the two primary structural materials of the composite, typically occupying a large volume fraction, but also play a crucial role in the performance of the CMC [344]. The features of the fibers are among the decisive factors affecting the strength, toughness, and antioxidation of the whole composite.

*
Fig. 31(a) Schematic structure of fiber-reinforced composites. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [329], © The Authors 2022. (b) SEM image showing constituents of SiCf/SiC composite with a chemical vapor-infiltrated (CVI) SiC matrix and Hi-Nicalon S SiC fibers coated with pyrolytic carbon. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [2], © Elsevier B.V. 2020.

In practice, CMC components prefer to be manufactured via net shape forming because their inherent high hardness and brittleness lead to large problems in later processes [7,357]. Hence, flexible ceramic fibers are typically woven or arranged into textiles or unidirectional prepregs and braided or laid into a designed preform, such as one-dimensional (1D) woven, two-dimensional (2D) woven, or three-dimensional (3D) woven. Then, the preform is densified to obtain CMCs with specific shapes via different methods. There are four main densification methods used to form the matrix [6]: CVI [358,359], precursor impregnation and pyrolysis (PIP) [345,360−362], melt infiltration (MI) [173,363,364], and nano infiltration and transient eutectic (NITE) [352,365−367]. Table 6 lists the basic structures and properties of recently reported SiCf/SiC composites. These four methods are quite different from each other and possess unique features. In practical production, CMCs are not always obtained through one of those methods. In some works, combining these four methods has also been adopted to increase density, shorten production periods, and reduce costs [368−371].

Apart from the reinforcing fiber and matrix, the interface plays a vital role in promoting the characteristics and performance of CMCs [372−375]. The functions of the interface are numerous, such as transferring the loads applied to the matrix to the fibers, protecting the fibers under a high-temperature environment, toughening the composite by deflecting the cracks and debonding [374], and relieving the thermal stress between the fibers and matrix due to their difference in thermal expansion coefficient. For SiCf/SiC composites, pyrolytic carbon (PyC) and hexagonal boron nitride (hex-BN) are commonly adopted as interfaces [376,377] and are deposited on fibers via CVI or CVD [375]. Moreover, interfacial coatings with multilayered structures, represented by (PyC-SiC)n* and (BN-SiC)*n*, are often considered owing to their increased toughening mechanism and enhanced oxidation resistance [374,378] and self-healing properties [379].

**Application in aero-engine and gas turbines**. With the rapid development of the aviation industry, new-generation aircraft urgently requires aero engines with larger thrusts, better fuel economy, and environmental friendliness [378,380]. An effective way is to increase the working temperature of engine hot parts [381−383]. The existing hot components of aero-engines, such as turbines and combustors, are usually composed of superalloys [384−386]. As the service temperature of sixth-generation nickel-based single-crystal superalloys is only approximately 1100 °C [387], complex structures with hundreds to thousands of air-cooling holes have been designed to dissipate heat and increase the working temperature [388−390], especially for well-designed turbine blades [381]. However, it has become increasingly difficult for high-temperature single-crystal alloys to meet newly promoted service temperature requirements. For example, according to the performance of the new fifth-generation fighter, the turbine inlet temperature is estimated to reach more than 2150 K [391], far beyond the working temperature of existing single-crystal alloys.

Facing this severe material problem, SiCf/SiC CMCs stand out among various high-temperature materials. SiCf/SiC CMCs overcome the brittleness of pure ceramics through composite toughening and inherit the oxidation and high-temperature resistance of SiC. The reported working temperature can be 100–300 °C higher than that of single-crystal alloys [339], while the density of SiCf/SiC CMC is approximately 3.2 g·cm−3, less than 1/3 the density of the alloys. Hence, SiCf/SiC CMCs can reduce the weight of the engine and improve the engine performance [61,380]. Therefore, SiCf/SiC CMCs have gained much attention from various countries and have been regarded as key materials for the next generation of aero engines, especially for nozzles, combustors, and turbine components [7,392−396], as shown in Fig. 32.

*
Fig. 32Application cases of SiCf/SiC composites for gas turbines and aero engine components. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [392], © The American Ceramic Society 2015; Ref. [395], © Deutsches Zentrum für Luft-und Raumfahrt e.V. 2020; Ref. [396], © Wiley-VCH Verlag GmbH & Co. KGaA 2014; Ref. [7], © Chinese Society of Aeronautics and Astronautics 2020.

Due to the great potential of SiCf/SiC CMCs for thermal and structural components, many countries, such as the USA and France, have started to research the application of SiCf/SiC CMCs to the hot components of gas turbines and aero-engines. In the 1990s, Snecma Propulsion Solide (SPS, now Safran Ceramics), a French company, benefited from its early experience in C/C composites [397] and manufactured the SiCf/SiC inner nozzle flap for the F53-2 engine [392]. A flap made of CMC achieved a weight reduction of 60% compared with that of cobalt-based alloys and held up well in a ground engine test for 350 h. Later, the Mirage 2000, equipped with an F53-2 engine with this CMC flap, took off at the Paris Air Show in 1989 [398]. Safran continued their research on new SiCf/SiC materials and invented a self-sealing matrix for SiCf/SiC composites [397]. Safran has continued its research on the application of SiCf/SiC composites and has made many achievements in manufacturing other SiCf/SiC components for various engines, such as combustor liners, turbine shrouds, and flame mixers.

Since the early 1990s, the US government has initiated many programs supporting CMC application research, such as the Enabling Propulsion Materials (EPM) program of NASA [399], the Ceramic Stationary Gas Turbine (CSGT) program of the US Department of Energy [400], the Continuous Fiber Ceramic Composite (CFCC) and Advanced Materials for Advanced Industrial Gas Turbines (AMAIGT) programs of the US Department of Energy [401,402], and the Integrated High-Performance Turbine Engine Technology (IHPTET) and Versatile Received Advanced Turbine Engines (VAATE) programs of the US Army [403,404]. Supported by those programs, GE, Solar Turbine, NASA Glenn Research Center (GRC), and United Technologies Research Center (UTRC) have carried out much research on applying SiCf/SiC CMCs in gas turbines and aero-engines. For example, a SiCf/SiC CMC combustor liner developed by UTRC and PW achieved a 30% reduction in NOx* at full power and a 20% reduction in CO at idle conditions during the test [405]. Solar Turbine developed an environmental barrier coating (EBC)-coated SiCf/SiC combustor liner for Solar Centaur 50S engines. It was found that the EBC-coated combustor liner reduced the production of NO*x* and CO and withstood the test duration for approximately 15,000 h [406]. The SiCf/SiC vane developed by NASA GRE completed a 50 h steady-state operation and 102 thermal cycles by a high-pressure burner rig (HPBR) [407,408].

Among those competitors, GE came to the forefront due to its remarkable contribution and achievement in applying SiCf/SiC CMCs. GE developed the prepreg-MI technique for producing SiCf/SiC CMCs and verified that the EBC coating prolonged the life of the SiCf/SiC CMCs. A SiCf/SiC liner with an EBC coating for a ceramic stationary gas turbine engine withstood ground testing for 12,822 h [409]. The EBC-coated SiCf/SiC turbine vane developed by GE was subjected to a cumulative ground test of 21,740 h with 126 start-stop cycles on a CE 7FA engine, a world record for continuous use of any CMC component [395,409].

The extensive experience of GEs in applying SiCf/SiC CMCs in gas turbines has contributed to the development of hot components for aero-engines. A series of vital SiCf/SiC components of turbines and combustors, such as F136, F414, LEAP, and GE9X, were developed for military and civil aeroengines. Some of the milestones in applying SiCf/SiC CMCs achieved by GE are discussed below. In 2010, the SiCf/SiC low-pressure turbine rotor blade for the F414 engine successfully finished the engine test, representing an important breakthrough in CMC-based components from static to rotating parts [395]. In 2015, the 1st HPT shroud, developed in cooperation with Safran, for the LEAP engine was certified by the EASA and FAA and later entered series production for the Airbus A320 neo, Boeing 737 Max airliner, and COMAC C919 [380,395]. In September 2020, GE9X, one of the most advanced civil aeroengines, obtained FAA certification and was the first engine with multiple CMC components, whose inner and outer combustor liners, 1st and 2nd HPT vanes, and 1st HPT shroud are all made of SiCf/SiC CMCs [393]. Hence, GE is a worldwide leader in the application of SiCf/SiC CMCs.

Other countries, such as Japan, have also launched research programs to promote their application of SiCf/SiC CMCs in the engine field [410−412]. In addition, Table 7 summarizes the research and application of SiCf/SiC components for gas turbines and aero-engines. Applying SiCf/SiC to a real engine takes years and is expensive. However, an increasing number of studies have focused on SiCf/SiC CMCs.
