**Applications in the nuclear industry**. SiCf/SiC composites are among the most promising structural materials for nuclear applications due to their low neutron activity, neutron radiation stability, and high-temperature stability [2,9,10,432]. The advantages of SiCf/SiC composites include their potential as structural and cladding materials for fusion reactors (FRRs), as shown in Fig. 33 [59,151,433]. In fusion reactor design, SiCf/SiC composites are mainly used for components such as first-wall structural materials, flow channel plug-ins, and cladding diverters. For example, Tauro blankets and SiCf/SiC composites, are used as first-wall cladding materials [434]. SiCf/SiC composites were used to manufacture the flow channel inserts (FCIs) of cladding in the EU power plant conceptual study (PPCS) project [435,436] and the US ITER Test Blanket Module (TBM) program [437]. SiCf/SiC composites were also selected as the structural materials for the deflector design for the US ARIES-CS reactor study [438]. Moreover, SiCf/SiC composites were developed as reactor core materials for light water reactors (LWRs) [10,439,440]. Kishimoto *et al*. [441] conducted irradiation experiments showing that NITE SiCf/SiC composites could be used as structural materials for pressurized water reactors (PWRs) operating under hydrogen-water chemical conditions.

*
Fig. 33SiCf/SiC composite cladding and SiCf/SiC composite control rods with articulating joints. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [151], © Elsevier B.V. 2022; Ref. [59], © Elsevier Ltd. 2012.

An investigation of the effect of irradiation on SiCf/SiC composites revealed that both the microstructure and chemical composition of the SiC fibers influence the strength of these composites under high-irradiation conditions [17]. Not all SiC fibers are suitable for application in the nuclear industry. For instance, only Hi-Nicalon S and Tyranno SA3 fibers are recognized as “nuclear grade”. The first-type SiC fibers (Nicalon fibers) and second-type SiC fibers (Hi-Nicalon fibers) exhibit significant volume shrinkage under low-dose neutron irradiation, leading to debonding from the expanding matrix and degrading the mechanical properties of the composites [442,443]. Sylramic fibers, as third-generation SiC fibers, are also not suitable because the B in the fibers can be easily transmuted into He upon irradiation. Third-generation Tyranno SA3 fibers exhibit structural stability under neutron irradiation due to their highly crystalline microstructure and large SiC grains [444,445]. It has been demonstrated that instead of Hi-Nicalon S fibers, Tyranno SA3 fiber-reinforced SiCf/SiC showed better stability after neutron irradiation up to 44 dpa at 590–640 °C [444]. Recently, with the appearance of Tyranno SA4 fibers [17,151,176,177], the initial higher interfacial shear stress (ISS) of Tyranno SA4 fiber-reinforced composites has allowed them to be more promising for use in environments with high neutron radiation [151].

Apart from the type of SiC fibers, the densification method and interfacial coating of SiCf/SiC composites significantly influence their radiation resistance. Currently, CVI and NITE processes are widely employed for fabricating nuclear SiCf/SiC claddings [441,444,446]. The high purity, crystallinity, and radiation stability of the CVI matrix make it suitable for nuclear application. With appropriate sintering aids, NITE enables rapid preparation of SiCf/SiC nuclear composites. It has been proven that interfaces such as PyC, BN, (SiC/BN)n*, and (SiC/PyC)*n* tend to fail due to abrupt changes in B or phase-induced volume variations in PyC [447−450]. Hence, optimizing the interface structure and exploring new interfaces are important [451−453]. Although numerous studies have been conducted on the preparation and evaluation of SiCf/SiC composites as nuclear structural materials, their practical application is still far from being realized. Many challenges related to material and engineering aspects, including joining techniques for nuclear SiCf/SiC composites [62,455,456], remain unresolved [454].

#### 5.1.2 MMCs

In contrast to PDSiC fibers with fine diameters, CVD-SiC fibers with large diameters are applied as reinforcing fibers for MMCs [93]. In MMCs, CVD-SiC fibers act as reinforcements and provide the main strength, while the metal matrix binds all the reinforcement materials in shape and transfers stress between them. Introducing SiC fibers into the metal matrix can enhance both the strength and service temperature, leading to weight reduction in comparison with that of pure metals [80,457]. Hence, SiC fiber-reinforced MMCs have attracted wide interest and are widely used in aerospace and weapons [458,459].

Due to the intrinsic differences between ceramics and metal matrices, the manufacturing of MMCs differs from that of CMCs. The processes are briefly summarized as liquid-phase methods and solid-state consolidation [460,461]. The former applies molten metal to fill the pores in the preforms of fibers, which is suitable for low melting point metals, such as Al. The latter combines fibers with solid metal powders, foils, fibers, or coatings and obtains composites after hot pressure, which is suitable for various metals. Until recently, the main research on the matrix of SiC fiber-reinforced MMCs has focused mainly on Al, Ti, and Ni [8,462−464]. SiCf/Al composites could be used for structural components of aircraft and missiles when the operating temperature does not exceed 500 °C (L-shaped reinforcement plates for aircraft and vertical tail fins for missile tails).

SiC fiber-reinforced Ti matrix composites (TMCs) have attracted much attention [35,81,464]. SiCf/Ti composites exhibit high specific strength, high specific stiffness, and excellent fatigue and creep resistance, making them ideal lightweight structural materials for aero-engines and aircraft structural materials (aircraft skins, bling, blades, and so on) [465,466], as shown in Fig. 34. SiCf/Ti composites can be used in the temperature range of 600–700 °C and can effectively reduce aircraft weight by replacing the original pure metal materials [80,467]. The USA’s National Air and Space Shuttle Program (NASP), the Integral High-Performance Turbine Engine Program (IHPTET), and the Titanium Matrix Composite Turbine Engine Components Consortium Program (TMCTECC) promoted the development of SiCf/Ti composites for aero-engine applications [468]. The UK’s Advanced Aero Engine Materials Program (ADAM) was dedicated to investigating SiCf/Ti composites as the next generation of MMCs. The French National Aerospace Center (ONERA) and the German Aerospace Center (DLR) also researched SiCf/Ti composites. According to Ref. [469], F-22 aircraft uses almost 40% of the SiCf/Ti composites in US fighter jets. In the integral leaf ring of the XTC16/1A engine, a Ti–6Al–4V alloy reinforced by SCS-6 SiC fibers was used as a structural material to achieve a 78% weight reduction of the component while still meeting service performance requirements [470].

Moreover, with the development of aerospace, traditional Ni-based high-temperature alloys have gradually failed to meet the new requirements of turbine discs (higher operating temperature and mass reduction) for aeroengines with high thrust-to-weight ratios. SiCf/Ni composites have become ideal for a new generation of aerospace materials, whose service temperature is expected to increase to 800–1000 °C. However, due to the violent interfacial reactions between Ni and SiC, SiCf/Ni composites are still stuck in the development of diffusion barrier coatings, and there are few reports on their performance and application [111,471,472].

#### 5.1.3 UHTCs

Currently, UHTCs are considered to be ideal materials for thermal protection in ultrahigh temperature areas, such as hypersonic vehicles (Fig. 35) [3,20,21,29,56,473]. When flying in the atmosphere, these vehicles generate aerodynamic heat. Their components, such as vehicle nose cones, wing leading edges, and engine hot ends, are exposed to severe high-temperature environments [20]. For example, in the European ATLLAS II project, when a hypersonic vehicle flew at a cruise Mach number of 5, the average surface temperature of the vehicle was approximately 1200–1300 K, and the temperature of the leading edge portion near the engine nacelle increased to approximately 1800 K. In particular, the surface temperature of the vehicle increased considerably when the leading edge was extremely thin (radius 20,21]. Therefore, thermal protection materials are needed to withstand high-temperature conditions of 2000–3000 °C.

![](https://wqketang.oss-cn-beijing.aliyuncs.com/zip-unzip/zip-c0c3641f-a96b-4598-afce-af9e73d471eb/JAC0936/files/JAC0936-35.jpg?Expires=1766505781&OSSAccessKeyId=STS.NZaSWrpFP6nuZWQLxNiDpx1od&Signature=8mnXlJhiWw9jdK54ArbSVFbxzkU%3D&x-oss-process=image%2Fresize%2Cm_fixed%2Cw_600&security-token=CAISywJ1q6Ft5B2yfSjIr5nUGO3GnZlxgayeWHHgqHgbZctcl%2FTEhjz2IHtKenhsBOsbtfk1mG5W5%2FgZlqJ9SptIAEfJa9d99MzfUJQhv9GT1fau5Jko1beHewHKeTOZsebWZ%2BLmNqC%2FHt6md1HDkAJq3LL%2Bbk%2FMdle5MJqP%2B%2FUFB5ZtKWveVzddA8pMLQZPsdITMWCrVcygKRn3mGHdfiEK00he8TouufTinpHMskGA1Aell7Mvyt6vcsT%2BXa5FJ4xiVtq55utye5fa3TRYgxowr%2Fwo0v0YpGya5YzHXwcPskvdKZbo78UqLQlla6w%2BGqFJqvPxr%2Fp8t%2Fx5fWJKAezhVgs8cVM8JOjIqKOscIsiJk4nzku%2BlV55c8Fdm%2BgUooJVgIMhTnduUfAPJAGOxzJitP%2BUVGGphr60TEnBL4rB5MUctfzRp2YQC8fUTzDnGoABTY0XsvWdY8LzFDdA%2F2hwFlLwSRnnzSYCvte8TKTahxt%2Bvolu05PooxBld5O2ctlrxI1rexU84fFrgLDzOFU73uwEa2TCBsaKEtzEihiPzmOCrbZyN44dNvlQPKfzOsbjfsPJ0Y%2Bjc%2BbaOPbxmj8V7bmqGmH4vX9JLuiJJ26Vbd0gAA%3D%3D)

In the 1980s, a research boom in hypersonic vehicles was launched in the USA [474]. In the early 21st century, Ames-Glenn Research Center, Sandia National Laboratories, and Air Force Space Command worked together to conduct flight demonstration tests of ultrahigh-temperature superconducting thermal protection structures [56]. The Ames-Glenn Research Center continued to explore strengthening and toughening techniques for ultrahigh-temperature materials. Fiber-reinforced UHTCs can overcome the inherent brittleness and poor thermal shock resistance of bulk ceramic materials. Reinforcing fibers are the main components of UHTCs and determine the mechanical properties and service temperature of composites [5].

Although carbon fibers are commonly used as reinforcing fibers for UHTCs [475], SiC fiber-reinforced UHTCs have been reported [476−479]. The strong interaction between the Hi-Nicalon fibers and the UHTC matrix reduced the overall performance of the composite [476]. Although the ultimate sintering temperature for Hi-Nicalon fibers to effectively toughen the composite was 1600 °C, the temperature could reach 1750 °C for Tyranno SA3 fiber-reinforced UHTCs. When UHTCs were reinforced by Tyranno SA3 fibers with high crystallinity, interactions between the SiC fibers and the matrix also occurred but were limited [477,478]. Some researchers have turned their attention to fabricating and applying ultrahigh-temperature ceramic fibers [256,258,259,271]. Matech Global Strategic Materials Inc. (Matech GSM), USA reported the development of ultrahigh-temperature ceramic fibers, such as HfC fibers, TaC fibers, and other ultrahigh-temperature ceramic fiber-reinforced UHTCs [3].

### 5.2 Catalyst carriers

Porous TiC nanofibers are considered ideal materials for catalysis. Studies of TiC fibers have focused mainly on the preparation of TiC/C fibers and their application [314−316,480]. TiC/C fibers have been investigated by several researchers as catalysts. Wei *et al*. [314] applied electrospinning to fabricate TiC/C nanofibers (80–200 nm), which could be used as ORR catalysts. The TiC/C nanofibers stabilized in air exhibited good redox properties, with onset potentials, peak potentials, peak current densities, and charge transfer numbers of 0.941 V vs. reversible hydrogen electrode (RHE), 0.812 V vs. RHE, 0.742 mA·cm−2, and 3.29, respectively.

Additionally, TiC/C fibers can be used as catalysts for the N2 reduction reaction (NRR). Yu *et al*. [315] used TiC/C nanofibers (300 nm) as catalysts for converting N2 to NH3. With 0.1 M HCl, the electrocatalyst obtained a large NH3 yield of 14.1 μg·h−1·* mgcat−1, and the Faraday efficiency was as high as 5.8% at −0.5 V compared with that of the reversible hydrogen electrode. The TiC nanocrystals in the TiC/C nanofibers provided strong N2 affinity, facilitating N≡N bond breaking, and the subsequent hydrogenation reaction.

Zhang et al*. [57] designed a highly active electrocatalyst for Li–S batteries by growing vertical graphene (VG) *in situ* while converting substrate TiO2 nanofibers into TiC nanofibers to obtain nanostructured VG/TiC electrocatalysts. The overall adsorption capacity of the electrocatalyst was important for Li–S batteries. The Brunauer–Emmett–Teller (BET) surface area of the VG/TiC nanofibers (145.4 m2·g−1) was much larger than that of the TiO2 nanofibers (18.4 m2·g−1). Additionally, to achieve highly improved redox kinetics, this active VG/TiC electrocatalyst was used as a sulfur body, significantly improving the performance of Li–S batteries with high sulfur loading and a low electrolyte/sulfur (E/S) ratio (Fig. 36(a)). It was further proven that VG/TiC nanofibers were among the best catalyst candidates for enhancing electrochemical reversibility.

*
Fig. 36(a) Schematic diagram showing growth process of VG/TiC electrocatalyst through electrospinning coupled with CVD. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [57], © The Authors 2021. (b) Schematic diagram of mechanism for simultaneous separation of photogenerated holes and electrons on mesoporous SiC NFs–Cx*. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [58] © Springer Nature 2016.

SiC materials with high specific surface areas meet the requirements of catalyst supports, which can better disperse the active components and increase the number of reaction sites [481]. In addition, SiC materials have a thermal conductivity of 146–270 W·m−1·K−1 (α-SiC) and unique photoelectric properties with an adjustable band gap range of 2.39–3.33 eV, making them ideal inert solids for catalysis [482−485]. SiC nanofibers are considered potential catalysts for hydrogen preparation and good catalyst carriers [58,483,486]. Wang *et al*. [58] reported the fabrication of mesoporous SiC nanofibers with *in situ* embedded graphite carbon (SiC NFs–C*x*) via a one-step CR between electrospun carbon nanofibers and Si powders. SiC NF–C*x* exhibited high hydrogen precipitation efficiency under simulated sunlight (180.2 μmol·g−1·h−1) and visible light irradiation (31.0 μmol·g−1·h−1) in a high-pH solution. As shown in Fig. 36(b), mesoporous SiC nanofibers with large aspect ratios provided more active sites and continuous transport paths for charge carriers, further improving the separation of electron–hole pairs.

### 5.3 Sensor

SiC materials are ideal broadband semiconductors for high-temperature sensors because of their wide bandgap, high carrier mobility, high electron saturation drift rate, high thermal conductivity, and good mechanical properties [487]. Among them, SiC nanomaterials have the advantages of a large aspect ratio and high specific surface area [488], providing more active sites for the target gas. Changes in the external environment can rapidly affect surface ion transport and thus electrical properties. In addition, the conventional solid structure limits chemically induced reactions at the material surface. Designing and fabricating SiC nanofibers with microscopically interconnected open pore structures can provide more efficient active sites and facilitate gas diffusion, thus further improving the sensing performance. Thus, SiC fibers can be used as gas sensors with a fast response, high sensitivity, and good selectivity, serving as ideal gas sensors in harsh environments [489]. High-performance gas sensors can be prepared using SiC fibers synergized with metal oxides (SnO2, ZnO, In2O3, TiO2, and NiO) due to their abundant oxygen vacancies, which are favorable for adsorbing target gases [60,489−492].

Wu *et al*. [60] fabricated novel hierarchical SiC nanofibers (HSiCs) constructed from interwoven SiC nanorods with a high surface area of 108.7 m2·g−1 and an interconnected porous structure. Then, Pt nanoparticles were uniformly deposited on the hierarchical SiC nanofibers (Pt/HSiC). The Pt/HSiC-based sensor showed a sensitivity of 9.1% and a short response/recovery time of 2/5 s when detecting 500 ppm NH3 at 500 °C. As shown in Fig. 37(a), the layered interconnected porous nanostructures mainly contributed to the shorter response/recovery time due to gas molecules easily diffusing to the active sites. The unique three-dimensional structure hindered the collapse of the scaffold, thus inhibiting the aggregation of active Pt nanoparticles and making the material reproducible. The deposition of Pt nanoparticles with a higher energy band (5.37 eV) prolonged the depletion layer and excited more oxygen ions at the surface compared to SiC with an energy band of 4.5 eV (Figs. 37(b) and 37(c)).

*
Fig. 37(a) Schematic of NH3 diffusion process in hierarchical SiC nanofibers; (b) energy band diagram for Pt and SiC; (c) schematic of proposed mechanism of NH3 sensing on HSiC and Pt/HSiC. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [60], © The Royal Society of Chemistry 2019.

A simple, high-performance gas sensor materials were designed by Wang et al*. [216], as shown in Fig. 38(a). SnO2 nanosheets were grown on SiC nanofibers to form a SnO2/SiC heterostructure (SnO2 NSs@SiC NFs). Experiments were conducted to test its response to different gases, including ethanol, methanol, hydrogen, isopropanol, acetone, and xylene, at 500 °C (Figs. 38(b)–38(f)). The materials showed excellent reproducibility, even at high temperature. The response time was less than 5 s, and the corresponding recovery time was less than 15 s. In particular, the SnO2 NSs@SiC NFs had good selectivity for ethanol, with response and recovery times of 4 and 6 s, respectively, for 100 ppm ethanol (Fig. 38(g)).

*
Fig. 38(a) Schematic of synthesis process for SnO2 NSs@SiC NFs. Ultrafast response/recovery performance of SnO2 NSs@SiC NF hierarchical composite sensor toward (b) methanol, (c) isopropanol, (d) acetone, (e) hydrogen, and (f) xylene. (g) Comparison of sensitivity to various gases, indicating high selectivity of sensor for ethanol. Gas concentration was 100 ppm, and operating temperature was 500 °C. Reproduced with permission from Ref. [216], © The Royal Society of Chemistry 2016.

Moreover, TiC exhibits electrochemical catalytic activity similar to that of precious metals but at a low price. Therefore, TiC fibers are used in biosensors [493−498]. Meanwhile, TiC is regarded as a reinforcing agent for composites with various polymers, improving the electrochemical properties and thermal stability. Thus, TiC fibers are used in capacitive humidity sensors [322,499,500]. Mazhar et al*. [322] fabricated polyvinyl alcohol (PVA)/TiC fibers for capacitive humidity sensors. The fabricated humidity sensor exhibited a short response/recovery time of 2.8/1.7 s and a high sensitivity of 1,277,355.0% at 100 Hz. High sensitivity and fast response or recovery were achieved over a wide humidity range of 15%–93% RH at room temperature and various frequencies. In particular, the capacitive response of the sensor was stable for a long period (30 d) and during repeated operations. Guo *et al*. [493] used a one-step method to fabricate TiC/C nanofibers with good conductivity and porosity. When applied in immobilizing and biosensing glucose oxidase (GOD), the nanofibers were conducive to electron transfer and GOD loading. The glucose biosensor had excellent sensing performance, showing a wide linear range (0.013–10.5 mM, *R*2 = 0.999) and a low detection limit (3.7 μM, S/N = 3).

### 5.4 Supercapacitors

TiC exhibits excellent thermal conductivity (6–31 W·m−1·K−1), electrical conductivity ((4–5)×103 S·cm−1), corrosion resistance, and good stability, and many studies have attempted to apply TiC and its composite fibers as supercapacitor electrode materials [267,274,278,316,501,502]. For example, Tsai and Mondal [267] reported a simple method for fabricating TiC nanofibers and investigated the electrochemical performance of the TiC nanofibers as supercapacitor anodes. The specific capacitance of the TiC nanofiber electrode was 193.4 F·g−1 at a scan rate of 5 mV·s−1. In addition, Ren *et al*. [316] varied the reaction atmosphere (Ar atmosphere and air) while fabricating TiC/C nanofibers (100 nm). When the current density was 0.1 A·g−1, the specific capacitances of the TiC/C nanofibers fabricated under Ar and air atmospheres were 77.8 and 130.0 F·g−1, respectively. After 25,000 cycles, the capacitance retention of the TiC/C nanofibers fabricated in an Ar atmosphere and air reached 98.9% and 93.0%, respectively. Hence, these TiC/C nanofibers showed excellent electrochemical stability. Similarly, NbC fibers are applied for high-power supercapacitors and high-energy battery electrodes [503].

## 6 Conclusions and outlook

With increasing demand for structural materials with high-temperature resistance in the fields of aircraft engines, aerospace vehicles, and nuclear reactors, carbide ceramic fibers have attracted great attention due to their advantages of high tensile strength, excellent high-temperature resistance, and good oxidation resistance. In recent decades, carbide ceramic fibers have been intensively developed, represented by silicon carbide ceramic fibers that have been produced in large quantities and successfully applied in various aircraft engines. It is worth noting that many efforts have been devoted to preparing transition metal carbide ceramic fibers with ultrahigh melting points (> 3000 °C), such as ZrC fibers, HfC fibers, and TaC fibers, which are regarded as optimal reinforcement materials for extreme heating environments. Furthermore, carbide ceramic fibers have been fabricated for use as catalyst carriers, sensors, and supercapacitors, achieving structure‒function integration of the fibers.

Although great progress in the fabrication and application of carbide ceramic fibers has been achieved, the following aspects are still unsatisfactory.

(1) The fabrication of SiC fibers is very costly. Furthermore, the tensile strength of the present SiC fibers is much lower than their theoretical strength.

(2) Researches on the fabrication of ultrahigh-temperature carbide ceramic fibers cannot meet the urgent requirements of actual application due to the absence of fine-diameter fibers.

(3) Industrializing the production of carbide ceramic nanofibers is difficult because the fabrication of these carbide nanofibers relies mainly on electrospinning.

Therefore, future research on carbide ceramic fibers should consider the following perspectives:

(1) The cost of the carbide ceramic fibers needs to be reduced. The high cost of carbide ceramic fibers could be attributed to two main factors: the high price of the precursor and the cumbersome process. For silicon carbide fibers, precursors such as PCS and PCS modified with heterogeneous elements are expensive. Similarly, the raw materials associated with the synthesis of precursors for high-temperature resistant carbide ceramic fibers, such as HfC and TaC, are costly. Therefore, the cost of fabricating carbide ceramic fibers, such as silicon carbide fibers, can be reduced by selecting or synthesizing other inexpensive raw materials as precursors, such as polysiloxanes, polysilazanes, polycarbosiloxanes, etc., and then synergistically modulating the process route to produce the fibers.

(2) The strength of the carbide ceramic fibers is expected to be enhanced. For instance, although fine-diameter SiC fibers presently exhibit a tensile strength of 3 GPa, significant advancements are still required to approach their theoretical value (above 40 GPa). Based on the current fabrication methods, it is urgent to optimize the process conditions to diminish microstructural defects and enhance the tensile strength. Additionally, reducing the fiber diameter could also help to enhance the tensile strength.

(3) New carbide ceramic fibers are to be developed. With the increasing demand for various carbide ceramic fibers, it is highly important to fabricate multifunctional fibers and ultrahigh-temperature carbide ceramic fibers with fine diameters. Moreover, complex-phase carbide fibers, which may exhibit excellent overall performance compared with single-phase carbide fibers, are worthwhile to consider.

(4) Theoretical system for carbide ceramic fibers should be established. With respect to the fabrication, characterization, and application of these fibers, it is necessary to set up a database including the elemental composition, microstructure, and basic properties, which would be helpful for understanding their relationships.

(5) The application of carbide ceramic fibers needs to be expanded. More attention should be given to exploring novel methods to prepare various composites reinforced by these fibers and improve their properties. Furthermore, with cost reduction in the future, these fibers could be applied in more fields than the ones mentioned above.
